\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{t,x}}\Big[\exp\big(\lambda(g-\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{t,x}}g)\big)\Big]\leq\exp\Big(\frac{CL^{2}}{2\gamma}\lambda^{2}\Big).

Apply this with
g=\tilde{a}
and
\lambda=r\geq 0
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{t,x}}\Big[\exp\big(r(\tilde{a}-\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{t,x}}\tilde{a})\big)\Big]\leq\exp\Big(\frac{CK^{2}}{2\gamma}r^{2}\Big).

Let
Y\sim\nu^{t,x}
, since
\|a-\tilde{a}\|_{\infty}\leq K
, we have
|a(Y)-\tilde{a}(Y)|\leq K
a.s. and

|\mathbb{E}a(Y)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{a}(Y)|\leq\mathbb{E}|a(Y)-\tilde{a}(Y)|\leq K
. Therefore

a(Y)-\mathbb{E}a(Y)=\big(\tilde{a}(Y)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{a}(Y)\big)+\big(a(Y)-\tilde{a}(Y)\big)+\big(\mathbb{E}\tilde{a}(Y)-\mathbb{E}a(Y)\big)\leq\big(\tilde{a}(Y)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{a}(Y)\big)+2K.

Exponentiating and taking expectations yields

\mathbb{E}\exp\big(r(a(Y)-\mathbb{E}a(Y))\big)\leq e^{2Kr}\mathbb{E}\exp\big(r(\tilde{a}(Y)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{a}(Y))\big)\leq\exp\Big(2Kr+\frac{CK^{2}}{2\gamma}r^{2}\Big).

∎

A.2.2
Bounds for Gaussian-mixture bridges

This subsection collects identities and estimates for the Gaussian-mixture bridges that underlie our flow-matching and diffusion-model constructions. In order to apply our Wasserstein discretization bounds, we need a linear growth estimate to ensure uniform moment bounds for the
continuous dynamics and its Euler scheme. The next lemma records yields a dimension-sharp affine bound on
\|v_{t}(x)\|
, which we will use
repeatedly to verify the linear-growth hypothesis in our numerical analysis.

Lemma 9
.

Let

X_{t}=f_{t}Y+g_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}\varepsilon,\qquad X_{0},\ \varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})\ \text{i.i.d. and independent of }Y\sim p^{\star}.

Suppose that
p^{\star}
is
(\alpha,\beta,K)
-weakly log-concave with
\alpha,K>0,\beta\in(0,1]
, write
p_{t}=\text{Law}(X_{t})
and

s_{t}(x):=\nabla\log p_{t}(x)
. Define the effective Gaussian scale

\bar{g}_{t}:=\sqrt{g_{t}^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}},\qquad a_{t}:=\frac{\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bar{g}_{t}},\qquad c_{t}:=f_{t}^{\prime}-a_{t}f_{t},\qquad\gamma_{t}:=\alpha+\frac{f_{t}^{2}}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}.

Then, for every
t
such that
f_{t},\bar{g}_{t}>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

v_{t}(x)=\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{f_{t}}x+\Big(\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}{f_{t}}-\bar{g}_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}\Big)s_{t}(x).

(38)

Moreover, supposing that
\|\arg\min u\|\leq A\sqrt{d}
, there exists a dimension-free constant

C>0
depending only on
\alpha,\beta,K,A
such that for all
t
and
x
,

\|v_{t}(x)\|\leq F_{t}\big(f_{t}\sqrt{d}+\|x\|\big),\qquad\text{with }F_{t}:=C\left(\left|\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{f_{t}}\right|+\frac{1}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\left|\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}{f_{t}}-\bar{g}_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}\right|\right).

Proof.

Fix
t
such that
f_{t},\bar{g}_{t}>0
and
f_{t}^{\prime},g_{t}^{\prime},\sigma_{t}^{\prime}
exist.
Introduce the Gaussian rotation

\xi_{t}:=\frac{g_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}\varepsilon}{\bar{g}_{t}},\qquad\eta_{t}:=\frac{\sigma_{t}X_{0}-g_{t}\varepsilon}{\bar{g}_{t}}.

Then
(\xi_{t},\eta_{t})
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, independent of
Y
, and

X_{t}=f_{t}Y+\bar{g}_{t}\xi_{t}.

Differentiating
X_{t}
yields

\dot{X}_{t}=g_{t}^{\prime}X_{0}+f_{t}^{\prime}Y+\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\varepsilon.

Moreover,

g_{t}^{\prime}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\varepsilon=\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}\xi_{t}+\frac{g_{t}^{\prime}\sigma_{t}-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}g_{t}}{\bar{g}_{t}}\eta_{t}.

Since
X_{t}
depends only on
(Y,\xi_{t})
, we have
\eta_{t}\perp X_{t}
and hence

\mathbb{E}[\eta_{t}|X_{t}]=0
. Therefore, by linearity of conditional expectation,

v_{t}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\dot{X}_{t}|X_{t}=x]=f_{t}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[Y|X_{t}=x]+\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[\xi_{t}|X_{t}=x].

Writing
\mu_{t}(x):=\mathbb{E}[Y|X_{t}=x]
and using
\xi_{t}=(X_{t}-f_{t}Y)/\bar{g}_{t}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[\xi_{t}|X_{t}=x]=\frac{x-f_{t}\mu_{t}(x)}{\bar{g}_{t}},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad v_{t}(x)=a_{t}x+c_{t}\mu_{t}(x).

Next, differentiating under the integral sign in the representation

p_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p(y)\varphi^{x,\bar{g}_{t}}(f_{t}y)dy

gives the standard score/posterior-mean identity

s_{t}(x)=\nabla\log p_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\big(f_{t}\mu_{t}(x)-x\big),\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\mu_{t}(x)=\frac{x+\bar{g}_{t}^{2}s_{t}(x)}{f_{t}}.

(39)

Plugging this into
v_{t}(x)=a_{t}x+c_{t}\mu_{t}(x)
yields

v_{t}(x)=\Big(a_{t}+\frac{c_{t}}{f_{t}}\Big)x+\frac{c_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}{f_{t}}s_{t}(x).

Since
a_{t}+c_{t}/f_{t}=f_{t}^{\prime}/f_{t}
and
c_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}/f_{t}=(f_{t}^{\prime}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}/f_{t})-\bar{g}_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}
,
we obtain (
38
).

We now prove the linear growth bound.
From (
39
),

\|s_{t}(x)\|\leq\frac{1}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\big(f_{t}\|\mu_{t}(x)\|+\|x\|\big).

Using Lemma
16
we get

\|\mu_{t}(x)\|\leq C\left(\big\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\big\|+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\gamma_{t}}}+\frac{f_{t}}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}}\|x\|\right).

Plugging this into the previous display yields

\|s_{t}(x)\|\leq\frac{C}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\left(f_{t}\Big(\big\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\big\|+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\gamma_{t}}}\Big)+\Big(1+\frac{f_{t}^{2}}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}}\Big)\|x\|\right)\leq\frac{C}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\big(f_{t}\sqrt{d}+\|x\|\big),

where we used Lemma
10
to bound
\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\|\lesssim\sqrt{d}
under

\|\arg\min u\|\leq A\sqrt{d}
, and that
f_{t}^{2}/(\bar{g}_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t})\leq 1
.
Finally, applying (
38
) and the last bound on
\|s_{t}(x)\|
gives

\|v_{t}(x)\|\leq\left|\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{f_{t}}\right|\|x\|+\left|\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}{f_{t}}-\bar{g}_{t}\bar{g}_{t}^{\prime}\right|\|s_{t}(x)\|\leq F_{t}\big(f_{t}\sqrt{d}+\|x\|\big),

with
F_{t}
as stated.
∎

Several estimates in the sequel
involve the auxiliary probability measure
\nu_{t,0}
obtained by tilting the base potential
u
with the
quadratic term coming from the Gaussian kernel at
x=0
. The quantity
\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\|
then appears
as a centering term in conditional-expectation bounds, and it must be controlled at the natural
\sqrt{d}

scale with constants independent of the ambient dimension. The next lemma provides precisely such a bound.

Lemma 10

(A
\sqrt{d}
bound on the mean under
\nu_{t,0}
)
.

Assume that
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and
\alpha
-strongly convex for some
\alpha>0
.
For
t\in(0,1]
, let
\nu_{t,0}
be the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with density

\nu_{t,0}(dy)=\frac{1}{Z_{t}}\exp\Big(-u(y)-\frac{\|f_{t}y\|^{2}}{2\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}\Big)dy.

Let
y_{0}:=\argmin u
and
\lambda_{t}:=\frac{f_{t}^{2}}{\bar{g}_{t}^{2}}
.
Then

\big\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\big\|\leq\|y_{0}\|+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\alpha+\lambda_{t}}}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we give the proof only in the
C^{2}
full-support case; the extension to the general case where
u=+\infty
outside a closed convex support is straightforward and follows from the same argument, with an additional boundary term of favorable sign.

Define

U_{t}(y):=u(y)+\frac{\lambda_{t}}{2}\|y\|^{2},\qquad y_{t}:=\argmin U_{t}.

The optimality conditions of
y_{0}
and
y_{t}
read
\nabla u(y_{0})=0
and
\nabla u(y_{t})+\lambda_{t}y_{t}=0
.
By convexity of
u
,

0\leq\langle\nabla u(y_{t})-\nabla u(y_{0}),y_{t}-y_{0}\rangle=\langle-\lambda_{t}y_{t},y_{t}-y_{0}\rangle=-\lambda_{t}\big(\|y_{t}\|^{2}-\langle y_{t},y_{0}\rangle\big).

Hence
\|y_{t}\|^{2}\leq\langle y_{t},y_{0}\rangle\leq\|y_{t}\|\|y_{0}\|
, so
\|y_{t}\|\leq\|y_{0}\|
. Since
\nabla^{2}U_{t}=\nabla^{2}u+\lambda_{t}\text{Id}\succeq(\alpha+\lambda_{t})I_{d}
,
the measure
\nu_{t,0}(dy)\propto e^{-U_{t}(y)}dy
is
(\alpha+\lambda_{t})
–strongly log-concave.
Using integration by parts for each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}\big[(W_{i}-y_{t,i})\partial_{i}U_{t}(W)\big]=1,

and summing over
i
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}\big[\langle W-y_{t},\nabla U_{t}(W)\rangle\big]=d.

(40)

By
(\alpha+\lambda_{t})
–strong convexity and
\nabla U_{t}(y_{t})=0
,

\langle y-y_{t},\nabla U_{t}(y)\rangle=\langle y-y_{t},\nabla U_{t}(y)-\nabla U_{t}(y_{t})\rangle\geq(\alpha+\lambda_{t})\|y-y_{t}\|^{2}.

Taking expectation and using (
40
) yields

d\geq(\alpha+\lambda_{t})\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}\|W-y_{t}\|^{2},\qquad\text{so}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}\|W-y_{t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{d}{\alpha+\lambda_{t}}.

Therefore,

\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]-y_{t}\|\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}\|W-y_{t}\|^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{d}{\alpha+\lambda_{t}}}.

Therefore,

\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]\|\leq\|y_{t}\|+\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{t,0}}[W]-y_{t}\|\leq\|y_{0}\|+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\alpha+\lambda_{t}}}.

∎

In our sampling guarantees we stop the continuous-time dynamics at some
\tau<1
, so we need to control
the early stopping error
W_{2}(\text{Law}(X_{1}),\text{Law}(X_{\tau}))
. The next Lemma provides a bound on this error in our setting.

Lemma 11

(Early stopping error)
.

Let
Y
be an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vector with
\mathbb{E}\|Y\|^{2}<\infty
and let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be deterministic.
Let
\eta
be an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vector such that
\mathbb{E}\|\eta\|^{2}<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0
and
\eta
is independent of
Y
.
Define

Z:=AY+\eta.

Then

W_{2}\bigl(\text{Law}(Y),\text{Law}(Z)\bigr)\leq\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl\|(I-A)Y\bigr\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\|\eta\|^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

Proof.

Consider the coupling
(Y,Z)
on the same probability space, where
Z=AY+\eta
.
By definition of the
2
-Wasserstein distance,

W_{2}^{2}\bigl(\text{Law}(Y),\text{Law}(Z)\bigr)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\text{Law}(Y),\text{Law}(Z))}\int\|x-x^{\prime}\|^{2}d\pi(x,x^{\prime})\leq\mathbb{E}\|Y-Z\|^{2},

since the law of
(Y,Z)
is an element of
\Pi(\text{Law}(Y),\text{Law}(Z))
.
Now,

Y-Z=(I-A)Y-\eta,

hence

\mathbb{E}\|Y-Z\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl\|(I-A)Y-\eta\bigr\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl\|(I-A)Y\bigr\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\|\eta\|^{2}-2\mathbb{E}\bigl\langle(I-A)Y,\eta\bigr\rangle.

Using the independence of
\eta
and
Y
and the assumption
\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0
(or more generally
\mathbb{E}[\eta\mid Y]=0
),

\mathbb{E}\bigl\langle(I-A)Y,\eta\bigr\rangle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\langle(I-A)Y,\mathbb{E}[\eta\mid Y]\bigr\rangle\Bigr]=0.

Therefore,

W_{2}^{2}\bigl(\text{Law}(Y),\text{Law}(Z)\bigr)\leq\mathbb{E}\|Y-Z\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl\|(I-A)Y\bigr\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\|\eta\|^{2},

and taking square roots yields the claim.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of the Lipschitz estimates

B.1
Preliminaries and notations

In this section, we introduce the posterior measures that naturally appear when differentiating the projected vector field
v_{t}
. The main point is that the derivatives of
v_{t}
can be written in terms of posterior covariances, which will be estimated in the next section.

For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, define the centering operator

H_{\mu}^{f}(z):=f(z)-\int f(z^{\prime})d\mu(z^{\prime}).

(41)

In particular,
H_{\mu}^{f}
is the fluctuation of
f
around its
\mu
-mean. Fix
t
with
\sigma_{t}>0
. Recall that in the probabilistic representation, one observes

X_{t}=m_{t}(Z)+\sigma_{t}\xi,

(42)

where
Z\sim\pi
and
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
is independent of
Z
. Hence, conditionally on
Z=z
,

X_{t}|(Z=z)\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathrm{Id}).

Recalling the notation
\varphi^{x,\sigma}
for the density of
\mathcal{N}(x,\sigma^{2}\text{Id})
, we define the probability measure

\pi^{t,x}(z):=\frac{\varphi^{m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}}(x)\pi(z)}{\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})},

(43)

which is the posterior law of
Z
given
X_{t}=x
, i.e.

\pi^{t,x}=\mathrm{Law}(Z\mid X_{t}=x).

With this notation, the differential of
v_{t}
can be expressed through posterior covariance terms.

Proposition 9
.

The differential of the vector field
v_{t}
from (
11
) is

\nabla v_{t}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z).

or equivalently

\nabla v_{t}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\mathrm{Var}_{Z|X_{t}=x}\big[m_{t}(Z)\big]+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\mathrm{Cov}_{Z|X_{t}=x}\big[m_{t}^{\prime}(Z),m_{t}(Z)\big].

The proof of Proposition
9
can be found in Section
B.4.1
. An important point is the probabilistic interpretation: the nontrivial terms in
\nabla v_{t}(x)
are posterior fluctuation terms under the law
\mathrm{Law}(Z\mid X_{t}=x)
.
It is convenient to push this posterior structure from the latent variable
Z
to the random mean
m_{t}(Z)
. For
\mu
a probability measure, define

Q_{t}\mu(x):=\int\varphi^{w,\sigma_{t}}(x)d\mu(w),

(44)

the Gaussian convolution of
\mu
at scale
\sigma_{t}
. In particular, if
\mu=(m_{t})_{\#}\pi
, then
Q_{t}\mu
is the density of
X_{t}
. Define the probability measure

p^{t,x}(w):=\frac{\varphi^{w,\sigma_{t}}(x)(m_{t})_{\#\pi}(w)}{Q_{t}(m_{t})_{\#\pi}(x)}.

(45)

This is precisely the posterior law of
m_{t}(Z)
given
X_{t}=x
:

p^{t,x}=\mathrm{Law}(m_{t}(Z)\mid X_{t}=x).

Thus, the variance term in Proposition
9
can be viewed as a covariance under
p^{t,x}
, which is often easier to estimate.
Let us take
u_{t}
strongly convex and
a_{t}

\beta
-Hölder as in Definition
1
such that

\exp(-u_{t}+a_{t})=(m_{t})_{\#}\pi.

Let

q_{t}(y):=\exp\left(-u_{t}(y)\right),

(46)

which is the log-concave part of
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi
. We then define

\nu^{t,x}(w):=\frac{q_{t}(w)\varphi^{w,\sigma_{t}}(x)}{Q_{t}q_{t}(x)}=\frac{\exp\left(-u_{t}(w)\right)\varphi^{w,\sigma_{t}}(x)}{\int\exp\left(-u_{t}(y)\right)\varphi^{y,\sigma_{t}}(x)dy},

(47)

which plays the role of a log-concave approximation of
p^{t,x}
. The probabilistic meaning is the same as above:
\nu^{t,x}
is the posterior law associated with the observation model

X_{t}=W+\sigma_{t}\xi

when the prior law of
W
has density proportional to
q_{t}
. Since
u_{t}
is strongly convex,
\nu^{t,x}
is strongly log-concave. This is exactly the setting where Brascamp-Lieb type inequalities provide sharp covariance bounds. Finally,
p^{t,x}
is a tilt of
\nu^{t,x}
by the perturbation
a_{t}
:

p^{t,x}(dw)=\frac{e^{a_{t}(w)}}{\int e^{a_{t}}d\nu^{t,x}}\;\nu^{t,x}(dw).

Therefore, the strategy in the next section is to first control covariances under the log-concave measure
\nu^{t,x}
, and then quantify the effect of the perturbation
e^{a_{t}}
.

B.2
Proofs of Theorem
1

B.2.1
General covariance bounds

This subsection gathers the covariance estimates that will be used in the proof of Theorems
1
.
The goal is to separate the different mechanisms contributing to the one-sided Lipschitz bound for
\nabla v_{t}(x)
. We use the notations of Section
B.1
.

A sharper bound under bounded perturbation.
Under the stronger bounded-perturbation regime of Assumption
11
below, one can remove the exponential prefactor in Theorem
1
; we record this variant now, and the rest of the section is devoted to the common proof of Theorems
1
and
7
.

Assumption 11
.

The probability density
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
(\alpha_{t},\beta,K_{t})
-weakly log-concave and writing

(m_{t})_{\#}\pi(x)=\exp(-u_{t}(x)+a_{t}(x))
, the
\beta
-perturbation
a_{t}
satisfies
\|a_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
.

Under the additional Assumption
11
we obtain the following refined bound.

Theorem 7
.

Let
v:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form (
11
) with
\sigma\in C^{1}_{\text{a.e.}}([0,1],[0,1])
and
(m_{t})_{t}
satisfying Assumptions
5
and
11
. Then for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,1]
such that
\sigma_{t}>0
, we have for all
\xi\in\{0,1\}
,

•

if
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
, then

\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)\leq C\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1},

•

if
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\geq 0
and
\nabla^{2}u_{t}\preceq A_{t}\text{Id}
in the decomposition
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi=\exp(-u_{t}+a_{t})
with
u_{t}
convex and
a_{t}

\beta
-Hölder (from Assumption
4
), then

\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)\leq C\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(L_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

with
C>0
depending only on
K_{1},\beta
and not the dimension
d
.

Compared to Theorem
1
, the structure of the bound is unchanged, but the uniform bound on
a_{t}
removes the
exponential envelope. This makes the estimate closer to the “intrinsic” scaling suggested by posterior concentration:
the sensitivity of
v_{t}
is attenuated by
(\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1)^{-1}
, while the score contribution retains the natural factor

\sigma_{t}^{\prime}/\sigma_{t}
.
We will not use this refined estimate in the remainder of the paper as our main focus is the unbounded weakly log-concave setting of Definition
1
, for which the perturbation is only assumed to be Hölder and may be unbounded. Theorem
7
is included only as a benchmark result: it shows that, under the stronger bounded-perturbation assumption, the exponential prefactor appearing in Theorem
1
can be removed, while preserving the same geometric scalings of the estimate.

We now prove Theorems
1
and
7
simultaneously. The two proofs follow the same lines, the only difference being that Theorem
7
uses the improved estimates available under Assumption
11
.

Beginning of the proof.

Let us first recall the formula given by Proposition
9
:

\nabla v_{t}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z).

Using the subadditivity of the maximum eigenvalue, we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)-\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(y)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(y)d\pi^{t,x}(y)\right)

(48)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}.

Equation (
48
) isolates two pieces:

•

the difference between the covariance under
p^{t,x}
and under
\nu^{t,x}
(this is the perturbative term);

•

the covariance under
\nu^{t,x}
alone (this is the log-concave core term).

We treat these two terms separately.

The log-concave core term.

The measure
\nu^{t,x}
is strongly log-concave because it combines the convex part of
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi
with a Gaussian likelihood.
The sign of
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}
determines which inequality is useful.

In the case where
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
, the prefactor
-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}/\sigma_{t}^{3}
is nonnegative, so an upper bound on the covariance matrix gives the desired estimate.
This is exactly the setting of the Brascamp–Lieb inequality.

Lemma 12
.

Let
\nu^{t,x}
the probability measure defined in (
47
). Suppose that
m
satisfies Assumption
4
, then for all
t>0
such that
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\leq\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

The proof of Lemma
12
can be found in Section
B.4.2
. The right-hand side is nonpositive when
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
, which reflects the fact that the strongly log-concave core contributes a contractive term in this regime.

In the case where
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\geq 0
, the prefactor changes sign, and the same covariance term may become expansive.
One now needs a lower bound on the covariance, which is obtained through a Cramér-Rao type inequality.
This explains the additional upper curvature assumption on
u_{t}
in Theorem
7
.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\nu^{t,x}
the probability measure defined in (
47
).
Suppose that
m
satisfies Assumption
4
and that
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi=\exp(-u_{t}+a_{t})
with
u_{t}
convex and
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\nabla^{2}u_{t}\preceq A_{t}\text{Id}
.
Then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t>0
such that
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have

\lambda_{\max}\left(-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{A_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

The proof of Lemma
13
can be found in Section
B.4.3
.

Comparing covariances under an exponential tilt.

We now turn to the first term in (
48
), namely the difference between the covariance under
p^{t,x}
and under
\nu^{t,x}
.
The next proposition expresses covariance comparison in terms of tilted moments of centered linear observables.

Proposition 10
.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
having second moments and such that
\frac{d\mu_{1}(y)}{d\mu_{2}}=e^{v(y)}
. Then, for all
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have

\displaystyle|h^{\top}

\displaystyle\left(\int H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{1}(y)-\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{2}(y)\right)h|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C|\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)|^{2}\ +|\int(h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y))^{2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)|.

The proof of Proposition
10
can be found in Section
B.4.4
. This proposition reduces the matrix-valued covariance comparison problem to scalar moment estimates, which can then be handled using functional inequalities for strongly log-concave measures.

From Hölder regularity to concentration.

The perturbation
a_{t}
is only assumed to be Hölder continuous, so we need an estimate that converts Hölder regularity into moment control under strong log-concavity.
This is the role of the generalized Poincaré inequality below.

Theorem 8

(Generalized Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that is
\gamma
-strongly log-concave for some
\gamma>0
.
Let
\alpha\geq 0
,
\beta\in(0,1]
and suppose
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\beta
-Hölder with constant
L>0
. Then,

\int\bigl|f-\int fd\mu\bigr|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq C_{\alpha}L^{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha\beta/2}.

The proof of Theorem
8
can be found in Section
B.4.5
. The scaling
\gamma^{-\beta/2}
is the key point: under
\gamma
-strong log-concavity, fluctuations occur at spatial scale
\gamma^{-1/2}
, and a
\beta
-Hölder function therefore fluctuates at scale
(\gamma^{-1/2})^{\beta}=\gamma^{-\beta/2}
.

Tilted moment estimates (bounded and unbounded perturbations).

We now apply Theorem
8
to the exponential tilt.
The next two lemmas give the estimates needed in Proposition
10
. The bounded perturbation case corresponds to Assumption
11
and yields the cleanest bound.

Lemma 14

(Covariance bounds with bounded perturbation)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a
\gamma_{t}
-strongly log concave measure with
\gamma_{t}>0
and
v\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
with
\|v\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
and
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}}\leq K_{t}
and such that
\int e^{v}d\mu=1
. Then, there exists
C>0
independent of the dimension
d
such that for all
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\xi\in[0,1]
and
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle|\int(h^{\top}H_{\mu}^{\text{Id}}(y))^{j}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu(y)|\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}\gamma_{t}^{-(j+\beta\xi)/2}.

The proof of Lemma
14
can be found in Section
B.4.6
. The parameter
\xi\in[0,1]
provides an interpolation between a rough bound (
\xi=0
) and a sharper Hölder-scale bound (
\xi=1
). This flexibility is useful later when optimizing the final estimate. Without boundedness of
v
, the same geometric scaling remains true, but one pays an exponential envelope.
This is the price of controlling the exponential tilt using only Hölder regularity.

Lemma 15

(Covariance bounds without bounded perturbation)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a
\gamma_{t}
-strongly log concave measure with
\gamma_{t}>0
and
v\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
with
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}}\leq K_{t}
and such that
\int e^{v}d\mu=1
. Then, there exists
C>0
independent of the dimension
d
such that for all
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\xi\in\{0,1\}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\Big|\int(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}(e^{v}-1)d\mu\Big|\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}\gamma_{t}^{-(j+\beta\xi)/2}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big).

The proof of Lemma
15
can be found in Section
B.4.7
.

Application to the posterior covariance comparison.

We now apply Proposition
10
with
\mu_{1}=p^{t,x}
and
\mu_{2}=\nu^{t,x}
, and then use Lemma
14
or Lemma
15
depending on whether Assumption
11
is available.
Since
\nu^{t,x}
is
(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})
-strongly log-concave, the previous estimates yield the following bound.

Proposition 11
.

Let
p^{t,x}
and
\nu^{t,x}
the probability measures defined in (
45
) and (
47
) respectively. Then, if
m
satisfies Assumption
4
, there exists
C>0
independent of the dimension
d
such that for all
\xi\in\{0,1\}
and
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

|h^{\top}\left(\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)h|\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{2+\beta\xi}{2}}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big).

If additionally
m
satisfies Assumption
11
,

|h^{\top}\left(\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)h|\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{2+\beta\xi}{2}}.

The proof of Proposition
11
can be found in Section
B.4.8
. This proposition is the main estimate for the covariance replacement of
p^{t,x}
by
\nu^{t,x}
in (
48
). After multiplying by
|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|/\sigma_{t}^{3}
, it produces the contribution involving
K_{t}
in the final one-sided Lipschitz bound.

The mixed covariance term and the role of Assumption
5
.

Let us now bound the other term appearing in Proposition
9
.
The next proposition controls the covariance between
m_{t}(Z)
and
m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)
under the posterior
\pi^{t,x}
.
The proof combines Cauchy–Schwarz with:

•

Assumption
5
, which controls the posterior variance of
m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)
;

•

the same covariance machinery as above, which controls the posterior variance of
m_{t}(Z)
.

Proposition 12
.

Supposing that
m
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have

|\int h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)|\leq CL_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big).

If additionally
m
satisfies Assumption
11
,

|\int h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)|\leq CL_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

The proof of Proposition
12
can be found in Section
B.4.9
.

B.2.2
Concluding the proof of Theorems
1
and
7

Let us prove Theorem
1
, the proof of Theorem
7
follows the same steps.
From Proposition
9
we have

\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right),

so we are going to bound the different terms of the right-hand side. We have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)-\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}.

Applying Proposition
11
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)

(49)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}K_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{2+\beta\xi}{2}}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}^{1-\beta\xi}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big),

(50)

and applying Proposition
12

we get for all
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

|\int h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)|\leq CL_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big).

In the case where
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}<0
, we get
by Lemma
12

\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\leq\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

so putting everything together we get

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}\right)\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

Now, in the case where
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}>0
, we get
by Lemma
13

\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{A_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\leq\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1},

so putting everything together we get

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(L_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}\right)\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

B.3
Technical novelty for one-sided Lipschitz estimates

An important conceptual point of this paper is that the relevant regularity object is not the terminal target density
p^{\star}
itself, but rather the family of intermediate center laws

\mu_{t}:=(m_{t})_{\#}\pi.

Indeed, once the Gaussian path is written as
X_{t}=m_{t}(Z)+\sigma_{t}\xi,

the Jacobian of the projected drift
v_{t}
is expressed through posterior fluctuation terms under
\mathrm{Law}(Z\mid X_{t}=x)
. Pushing this posterior from the latent variable
Z
to the random center
m_{t}(Z)
leads to the measure
p^{t,x}=\mathrm{Law}(m_{t}(Z)\mid X_{t}=x),

so that the key quantities are governed by the geometry of
\mu_{t}
, and not directly by that of
p^{\star}
. Writing

\mu_{t}=\exp(-u_{t}+a_{t}),

the proof then compares
p^{t,x}
with the log-concave surrogate
\nu^{t,x}
built from the convex part
u_{t}
, while the perturbation
a_{t}
is treated as an exponential tilt. In this way, the Gaussian noise is handled explicitly, and the nontrivial regularity input is precisely the weak log-concavity and Hölder control of
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi
. For the concrete diffusion and flow-matching models considered here, the assumptions on
p^{\star}
are used only to verify that these intermediate laws
\mu_{t}
enjoy the required structure. This is the reason why the argument is both model-independent and dimension-free at the level of the one-sided Lipschitz estimate.

Due to the large number of works we only compare the proof techniques with the closest to this paper, namely
7
and
30
. The main new technical ingredient is the covariance comparison under an Hölder unbounded exponential tilt, achieved through Theorem
8
+ Lemma
6
+ Lemma
15
; this replaces the transport/pointwise-Hölder route used in prior works.

Compared with
7
, the overlap is mainly at the level of the first reduction: in both arguments one introduces a strongly log-concave surrogate posterior
\nu^{t,x}
and exploits Brascamp-Lieb on that reference measure. The proof of Theorem
1
then departs from this scheme at the covariance comparison step between
p^{t,x}
and
\nu^{t,x}
. In
7
they use for
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\langle h,\text{Cov}_{p^{t,x}}h\rangle\leq\Big(\sqrt{\langle h,\text{Cov}_{\nu^{t,x}}h\rangle}+W_{2}(p^{t,x},\nu^{t,x})\Big)^{2},

and then invoke a
L^{\infty}
-transport estimate to bound the Wasserstein term.
Rather than transferring the covariance bound through a transport estimate, we work directly with covariance identities under exponential tilts. This requires a different input, namely quantitative moment bounds for Hölder observables under strongly log-concave measures, which are provided here by Theorem
8
and Lemma
6
/
15
. These bounds make it possible to control the effect of a merely Hölder perturbation on the posterior covariance. In addition, because the target quantity is a one-sided Lipschitz bound, the core covariance term has to be treated differently according to the sign of the noise derivative, leading to a Brascamp-Lieb bound in one regime and a Cramér-Rao bound in the other. This is the main structural difference with the log-Lipschitz perturbative argument of Brigati and Pedrotti.

Compared with
30
, the proof is reorganized so as to avoid the step where one uses the Hölder bound in a direct pointwise way. In that paper, the covariance difference is expanded first, and the Hölder bound is then used directly in pointwise form,

|a(x)-a(y)|\leq K\|x-y\|^{\beta},

which is precisely where dimension dependence enter.
Here the strategy is different. The proof first rewrites the covariance difference in terms of scalar tilted moments of centered linear observables, and only then uses Hölder regularity through dimension-free concentration for the strongly log-concave reference measure.

The same reorganization is also what makes the unbounded case manageable: the growth of the tilt is not treated at the first comparison step, but later, at the level of these scalar moments. More precisely, Lemma
6
shows that if
\mu
is a
\gamma
-strongly log-concave probability measure and
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
\beta
-Hölder function with Hölder constant
K
satisfying
\int e^{v}d\mu=1,

then

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq\exp\!\left(CK\gamma^{-\beta/2}+CK^{2}\gamma^{-\beta}\right)-1.

Thus, even when the tilt is unbounded, one still gets a dimension-free control of the exponential weight
e^{v}
. This rough variance bound is then sharpened in the proof of Lemma
15
by decomposing
e^{v}

into a normalized Lipschitz part and a uniformly small multiplicative error, which removes the leading linear term and yields a quadratic gain.

Finally, the argument must also handle flow-matching schedules, including the regime where the noise may vanish at the initial time. These changes in the argument are what allows one to keep dimension-free constants, to treat unbounded perturbations, and to handle both diffusion and flow-matching regimes within the same scheme.

B.4
Details for one-sided Lipschitz estimates

B.4.1
Proof of Proposition
9

Proof.

From (
11
) we have

\displaystyle\nabla v_{t}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}+\int\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(x-m_{t}(z))+m_{t}^{\prime}(z)\right)\frac{\nabla_{x}\varphi^{m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z)}{\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-\int\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(x-m_{t}(z))+m_{t}^{\prime}(z)\right)\frac{\varphi^{m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z)}{\left(\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})\right)^{2}}\nabla_{x}\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}+\int\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(x-m_{t}(z))+m_{t}^{\prime}(z)\right)\left(\frac{-(x-m_{t}(z))}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\frac{\varphi^{m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z)}{\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-\int\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(x-m_{t}(z))+m_{t}^{\prime}(z)\right)\frac{\varphi^{m_{t}(z),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z)}{\left(\int\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})\right)^{2}}\int\left(\frac{-(x-m_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\varphi^{m_{t}(z^{\prime}),\sigma_{t}}(x)d\pi(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\left(\int(x-m_{t}(z))^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)-\left(\int(x-m_{t}(z))d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)^{\otimes 2}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\left(\int m_{t}^{\prime}(z)(x-m_{t}(z))d\pi^{t,x}(z)-\int m_{t}^{\prime}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\int(x-m_{t}(z))d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int(x-m_{t}(z))\left(x-m_{t}(z)-(x-\int m_{t}(z^{\prime}d\pi^{t,x}(z^{\prime}))\right)d\pi^{t,x}(z)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int\left(m_{t}^{\prime}(z)-\int m_{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})d\pi^{t,x}(z^{\prime})\right)(m_{t}(z)-x)d\pi^{t,x}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z).

∎

B.4.2
Proof of Lemma
12

Proof.

As the probability density
\frac{q}{\int q}
is
\alpha_{t}
-log concave, we have that

\nu^{t,x}
is
(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})
-log-concave.
Then, using the Brascamp-Lieb inequality applied to functions of the form
x\mapsto\langle x,w\rangle
with
w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle w,\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y),w\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\langle w,H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}w\rangle d\nu^{t,x}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\langle H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)w\rangle^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\langle z-\int z^{\prime}d\nu^{t,x}(z^{\prime}),w\rangle^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-1}.

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}-1)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

∎

B.4.3
Proof of Lemma
13

Proof.

Recall that
\nu^{t,x}
has density proportional to

y\mapsto\exp\left(-u_{t}(y)-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)=:\exp\big(-V_{t,x}(y)\big).

As
\nabla^{2}u_{t}\preceq A_{t}\text{Id}
,

\nabla^{2}V_{t,x}(y)=\nabla^{2}u_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\text{Id}\preceq\Big(A_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big)\text{Id}.

Denote by
J
the Fisher information matrix of
\nu^{t,x}
,

J:=\int\nabla^{2}V_{t,x}(y)d\nu^{t,x}(y).

By the Cramer-Rao inequality, we have

\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)=\mathrm{Cov}_{\nu^{t,x}}(Y)\succeq J^{-1},

hence for any
w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\Big\langle w,\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)w\Big\rangle\geq\Big(A_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big)^{-1}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(-\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{\min}\left(\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\Big(A_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{A_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

Therefore,

\lambda_{\max}\left(-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H^{\text{Id}}_{\nu^{t,x}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\leq-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\cdot\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{A_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}=\frac{\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A_{t}}{A_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

∎

B.4.4
Proof of Proposition
10

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{1}(y)-\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\left(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}e^{v(y)}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\right)d\mu_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\left(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\right)e^{v(y)}d\mu_{2}(y)+\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\left(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\right)\left(e^{v(y)}-1+1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)+\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3},

(51)

with

A_{1}:=\int\left(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\right)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y),

A_{2}:=\int\left(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\right)d\mu_{2}(y)

and

A_{3}:=\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y).

Now,

\displaystyle H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\otimes\int zd\mu_{1}(z)+\left(\int zd\mu_{2}(z)-\int zd\mu_{1}(z)\right)\otimes y+H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\otimes\int zd\mu_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\otimes\left(\int zd\mu_{1}(z)-\int zd\mu_{2}(z)\right)+\left(\int zd\mu_{2}(z)-\int zd\mu_{1}(z)\right)\otimes H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)+\int zd\mu_{1}(z)-\int zd\mu_{2}(z)\right)\otimes\left(\int zd\mu_{1}(z)-\int zd\mu_{2}(z)\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\int zd\mu_{2}(z)-\int zd\mu_{1}(z)\right)\otimes H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)

and

\displaystyle\int zd\mu_{2}(z)-\int zd\mu_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(z)d\mu_{2}(z)-\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(z)d\mu_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(z)\left(1-e^{v(z)}\right)d\mu_{2}(z).

Then, writing
A:=\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(z)\left(1-e^{v(z)}\right)d\mu_{2}(z)
, we have

\displaystyle H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}=H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\otimes A+A^{\otimes 2}+A\otimes H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y).

Therefore, for
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have

\displaystyle|h^{\top}A_{2}h|

\displaystyle=|h^{\top}\int(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2})d\mu_{2}(y)h|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2}+2|\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)d\mu_{2}(y)\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2}

as

\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)d\mu_{2}(y)=0

and

\displaystyle|h^{\top}A_{1}h|

\displaystyle=|h^{\top}\int(H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}-H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2})\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)h|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2}|\int\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)|

\displaystyle+2\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2},

as
\int e^{v(y)}d\mu_{2}(y)=1
. Then, from (
B.4.4
) we have

\displaystyle|h^{\top}

\displaystyle\left(\int H_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{1}(y)-\int H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\mu_{2}(y)\right)h|

\displaystyle=|h^{\top}(A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3})h|

(52)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\left(\int h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+|\int(h^{\top}H_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{Id}}(y))^{2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu_{2}(y)|.

(53)

∎

B.4.5
Proof of Theorem
8

Proof.

Fix
\alpha\geq 2
and
r>0
. Set

L^{\prime}:=Lr^{\beta-1}

and define

\tilde{g}_{r}(x):=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl\{f(y)+L^{\prime}\|x-y\|\bigr\}.

By Lemma
5
, we have that
\tilde{g}_{r}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz and
\|f-\tilde{g}_{r}\|_{\infty}\leq Lr^{\beta}
. Let

m:=\int fd\mu,\qquad m_{r}:=\int\tilde{g}_{r}d\mu.

Then

f-m=(f-\tilde{g}_{r})+(\tilde{g}_{r}-m_{r})+(m_{r}-m),

so
we obtain

\int|f-m|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq 3^{\alpha-1}\Bigl(\int|f-\tilde{g}_{r}|^{\alpha}d\mu+\int|\tilde{g}_{r}-m_{r}|^{\alpha}d\mu+|m_{r}-m|^{\alpha}\Bigr).

(54)

We now bound each term on the right-hand side.

Approximation terms.

From
|f(x)-\tilde{g}_{r}(x)|\leq Lr^{\beta}
we deduce

\int|f-\tilde{g}_{r}|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq(Lr^{\beta})^{\alpha}.

Furthermore,

|m_{r}-m|=\Bigl|\int(\tilde{g}_{r}-f)d\mu\Bigr|\leq\int|f-\tilde{g}_{r}|d\mu\leq Lr^{\beta},

so

|m_{r}-m|^{\alpha}\leq(Lr^{\beta})^{\alpha}.

Lipschitz term.

The function
\tilde{g}_{r}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz so applying
Lemma
4
with
g=\tilde{g}_{r}

yields

\int|\tilde{g}_{r}-m_{r}|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq C_{\alpha}L^{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha/2}r^{\alpha(\beta-1)}.

Plugging the bounds in (
54
), we obtain

\int|f-m|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq 3^{\alpha-1}L^{\alpha}\Bigl(2r^{\alpha\beta}+C_{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha/2}r^{\alpha(\beta-1)}\Bigr).

We now choose
r
so as to balance the two terms in parentheses.
Solving
r^{\alpha\beta}=\gamma^{-\alpha/2}r^{\alpha(\beta-1)}
gives
r:=\gamma^{-1/2}.

With this choice,
we get

2r^{\alpha\beta}+C_{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha/2}r^{\alpha(\beta-1)}\leq C^{\prime}_{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha\beta/2}

for
C^{\prime}_{\alpha}=2+C_{\alpha}
. We conclude that

\int|f-m|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq 3^{\alpha-1}C^{\prime}_{\alpha}L^{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha\beta/2}.

Absorbing the factor
3^{\alpha-1}
into the constant, we obtain

\int\bigl|f-\int fd\mu\bigr|^{\alpha}d\mu\leq C_{\alpha}L^{\alpha}\gamma^{-\alpha\beta/2}.

Now the case
0<\alpha<2
is immediate from Hölder’s inequality.
∎

B.4.6
Proof of Lemma
14

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle|\int(h^{\top}H_{\mu}^{\text{Id}}(y))^{j}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\mu(y)|=|\text{Cov}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H_{\mu}^{\text{Id}}(Y))^{j},e^{v(Y)}\big)|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H_{\mu}^{\text{Id}}(Y))^{j}\big)\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v(Y)}\big)}.

Now applying Brascamp-Lieb inequality we have

\text{Var}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H_{\mu}^{\text{Id}}(Y))^{j}\big)\leq C\gamma_{t}^{-j}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle|e^{v(x)}-e^{v(y)}|\leq e^{K_{1}}|v(x)-v(y)|\leq e^{K_{1}}(2K_{1})^{1-\xi}|v(x)-v(y)|^{\xi}\leq e^{K_{1}}(2K_{1})^{1-\xi}K_{t}^{\xi}\|x-y\|^{\beta\xi}

so
e^{v}
is
\xi\beta
-Hölder with constant
CK_{t}^{\xi}
. If
\xi=0
, then

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v(Y)})\leq\int e^{2v(y)}\,d\mu(y)\leq e^{2K_{1}}.

If
\xi\in(0,1]
, then from Theorem
8
, we have

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v(Y)})\leq CK_{t}^{2\xi}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta\xi},

which gives the result.
∎

B.4.7
Proof of Lemma
15

Proof.

Fix
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
. Since
\int e^{v}d\mu=1
,

\int(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}(e^{v}-1)d\mu=\text{Cov}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j},e^{v}\big).

By Cauchy-Schwarz,

\Big|\int(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}(e^{v}-1)d\mu\Big|\leq\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}\big)}\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})}.

(55)

Let
V
be the potential of
\mu
, i.e.
d\mu(x)\propto e^{-V(x)}dx
, with
\nabla^{2}V\succeq\gamma_{t}I
.
The Brascamp-Lieb inequality yields for all
g\in C^{1}

\text{Var}_{\mu}(g)\leq\int\langle(\nabla^{2}V)^{-1}\nabla g,\nabla g\rangle d\mu\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{t}}\int\|\nabla g\|^{2}d\mu.

For
j=1
, take
g(x)=h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu}(x)
, so
\nabla g=h
and

\text{Var}_{\mu}(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{t}}.

For
j=2
, take
g(x)=(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu}(x))^{2}
, so
\nabla g=2(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu}(x))h
and

\text{Var}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{2}\big)\leq\frac{4}{\gamma_{t}}\int(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{2}d\mu=\frac{4}{\gamma_{t}}\text{Var}_{\mu}(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})\leq\frac{4}{\gamma_{t}^{2}}.

Hence in both cases
j\in\{1,2\}
we have

\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\mu}\big((h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}\big)}\leq C\gamma_{t}^{-j/2}.

(56)

Case
\boldsymbol{\xi=0}
.

Lemma
6
with
r=\gamma_{t}^{-1/2}
gives

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq\exp\Big(4K_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta/2}+CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\Big)-1.

Using
4u\leq 2u^{2}+2
with
u=K_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta/2}
, we obtain

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq\exp\big(C+CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big)-1\leq C\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big).

Taking square-roots and inserting into (
55
) together with (
56
)
gives the result for
\xi=0
.

Case
\boldsymbol{\xi=1}
.

Let

r:=\gamma_{t}^{-1/2},\qquad L:=K_{t}r^{\beta-1}=K_{t}\gamma_{t}^{(1-\beta)/2},\qquad\varepsilon:=K_{t}r^{\beta}=K_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta/2}.

Define the inf-convolution approximation

\tilde{v}(x):=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\{v(y)+L\|x-y\|\Big\}.

By Lemma
5
:
(i)
\tilde{v}
is
L
-Lipschitz;
(ii)
\|\tilde{v}-v\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
;
(iii)
\tilde{v}\leq v
pointwise. Consequently, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

0\leq\delta(x):=v(x)-\tilde{v}(x)\leq\varepsilon.

(57)

Set

c:=\log\int e^{\tilde{v}}d\mu\quad\text{ and }\quad w:=\tilde{v}-c.

Then
\int e^{w}d\mu=1
and
w
is
L
-Lipschitz. Moreover, since

\int e^{v}d\mu=1
, we have

-\varepsilon\leq c\leq 0.

(58)

Now decompose

e^{v}=e^{\tilde{v}+\delta}=e^{w}e^{c+\delta}

and define
X:=e^{w}
,
Z:=e^{c+\delta}
so that
e^{v}=XZ
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X]=1
.
From (
57
) and (
58
), we have

-\varepsilon\leq c+\delta\leq\varepsilon\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad e^{-\varepsilon}\leq Z\leq e^{\varepsilon}.

(59)

A product variance bound.

If
\mathbb{E}[X]=1
and
|Z|\leq b
a.s., then

\text{Var}(XZ)\leq 2b^{2}\text{Var}(X)+2\text{Var}(Z).

(60)

Indeed, write
XZ-\mathbb{E}[XZ]=U+V
with

U:=((X-1)Z-\mathbb{E}[(X-1)Z])
and
V:=Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]
.
Then
\text{Var}(XZ)=\mathbb{E}[(U+V)^{2}]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[U^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[V^{2}]=2\text{Var}(U)+2\text{Var}(Z)
.
Moreover,
\text{Var}(U)\leq\mathbb{E}[((X-1)Z)^{2}]\leq b^{2}\mathbb{E}[(X-1)^{2}]=b^{2}\text{Var}(X)
,
which gives (
60
).

Apply (
60
) with
b=e^{\varepsilon}
(by (
59
)):

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq 2e^{2\varepsilon}\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{w})+2\text{Var}_{\mu}(Z).

(61)

Bound on
\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{w})
.

Since
w
is
L
-Lipschitz and
\mu
is
\gamma_{t}
-strongly log-concave, we get applying Lemma
3
that there exists a universal
c_{0}>0
such that for all

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\int\exp\big(\lambda(w-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}w)\big)d\mu\leq\exp\Big(\frac{c_{0}L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\Big).

(62)

Let
m:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[w]
. Since
\int e^{w}d\mu=1
, Jensen gives
m\leq 0
and thus
e^{2m}\leq 1
.
Taking
\lambda=2
in (
62
) yields

\int e^{2(w-m)}d\mu\leq\exp\Big(\frac{2c_{0}L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\Big),

hence

\int e^{2w}d\mu=e^{2m}\int e^{2(w-m)}d\mu\leq\exp\Big(\frac{2c_{0}L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\Big).

Therefore,

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{w})=\int e^{2w}d\mu-1\leq\exp\Big(\frac{2c_{0}L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\Big)-1\leq C\frac{L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\exp\Big(C\frac{L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}\Big),

using
e^{x}-1\leq xe^{x}
for
x\geq 0
. Since
L=K_{t}\gamma_{t}^{(1-\beta)/2}
, we have

\frac{L^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}=K_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta},

and thus

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{w})\leq CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big).

(63)

Bound
\text{Var}_{\mu}(Z)
.

From (
59
),
Z\in[e^{-\varepsilon},e^{\varepsilon}]
. By Popoviciu’s inequality,

\text{Var}_{\mu}(Z)\leq\frac{(e^{\varepsilon}-e^{-\varepsilon})^{2}}{4}=\sinh(\varepsilon)^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}e^{2\varepsilon},

since
\sinh(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon e^{\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
.
Thus

\text{Var}_{\mu}(Z)\leq\varepsilon^{2}e^{2\varepsilon}.

(64)

Combining (
61
), (
63
), and (
64
), we get

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}e^{2\varepsilon}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big)+C\varepsilon^{2}e^{2\varepsilon}.

Since
\varepsilon^{2}=K_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}
and
e^{2\varepsilon}\leq e\exp(\varepsilon^{2})

(using
2\varepsilon\leq 1+\varepsilon^{2}
), we can absorb the
e^{2\varepsilon}
factors into

\exp(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta})
to obtain

\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})\leq CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big).

(65)

Taking square roots gives

\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\mu}(e^{v})}\leq CK_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta/2}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big).

(66)

Inserting (
66
) and (
56
) into (
55
), we get

\Big|\int(h^{\top}H^{\text{Id}}_{\mu})^{j}(e^{v}-1)d\mu\Big|\leq\big(C\gamma_{t}^{-j/2}\big)\big(CK_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta/2}e^{CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}}\big)=CK_{t}\gamma_{t}^{-(j+\beta)/2}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{-\beta}\big),

which gives the result for
\xi=1
.
∎

B.4.8
Proof of Proposition
11

Proof.

Suppose first that
\|a_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
. Let

v(y):=a_{t}(y)+\log\left(\int\exp\left(-u_{t}(w)\right)\varphi^{x,\sigma_{t}}(w)dw\right)-\log\left(\int\exp\left(a_{t}(w)-u_{t}(w)\right)\varphi^{x,\sigma_{t}}(w)dw\right),

so
p^{t,x}=e^{v}\nu^{t,x}
.
Combining Proposition
10
and Lemma
14
we have

\displaystyle|h^{\top}\left(\int H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}(y)-\int H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)^{\otimes 2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\right)h|

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(|\int h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y)\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\nu^{t,x}(y)|^{2}

(67)

\displaystyle+|\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(y))^{2}\left(e^{v(y)}-1\right)d\nu^{t,x}(y)|\Bigl)

\displaystyle\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{1+\beta\xi}{2}}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}+CK_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{2+\beta\xi}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq CK_{t}^{\xi}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\frac{2+\beta\xi}{2}}.

(68)

The unbounded case follows the same steps by replacing Lemma
14
by Lemma
15
.
∎

B.4.9
Proof of Proposition
12

Proof.

Suppose first that
\|a_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
. We have

\displaystyle|\int h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)^{1/2}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)=

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)-\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)+\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)

and applying Brascamp-Lieb inequality we have

\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)\leq(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-1}.

(69)

Then, from Proposition
11
we obtain

\displaystyle|\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)-\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)|\leq C\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

(70)

Finally, using Assumption
5
we have

\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)\leq L_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}

so we deduce that

\displaystyle\left(\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)^{1/2}\leq CL_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}.

The unbounded case follows the same steps.
∎

B.4.10
Proof of Theorem
2

Proof.

Fix
z\in[0,1]
. By Assumption
6
and Theorem
1
, for a.e.
t\in[\delta,1]
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)\leq\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\Bigg(C(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}})\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)+\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}\Bigg).

For
t\geq\delta
, Assumption
6
gives
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
, hence
\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}\leq 0
and we can drop this term.
Set, for
t\in[\delta,1]
,

g_{1}(t):=\frac{C}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\Bigg(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}\Bigg)\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big),

so that
\sup_{x}\lambda_{\max}(\nabla v_{t}(x))\leq g_{1}(t)
for a.e.
t\in[\delta,1]
.
We take
\xi_{t}=\mathds{1}_{t\geq s}
and split the integral accordingly:

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{1}g_{1}(t)dt

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\delta}^{1}\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)dt-\int_{\delta\wedge s}^{s}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)dt

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\int_{s\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)dt.

Since
(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\leq\alpha_{t}^{-\beta}
, the assumption

\forall t\in[0,\delta\vee s],\qquad K_{t}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{-\beta}\leq A

yields, for all
t\in[0,\delta\vee s]
,

\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)\leq e^{CA}.

(71)

Furthermore, on
[s\vee\delta,1]
we use
K_{t}\leq A\sigma_{t}^{-\chi}

and
(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\leq\sigma_{t}^{2\beta}
to get

\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)\leq\exp\big(CA^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2(\beta-\chi)}\big)\leq e^{CA^{2}},

where we used
\beta>\chi
and
\sigma_{t}\leq 1
.

On
[\delta\wedge s,s]\subset[0,\delta\vee s]
,
using (
71
),
\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\leq 1
, and
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
on
[\delta,1]
, we obtain

\displaystyle-\int_{\delta\wedge s}^{s}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)dt

\displaystyle\leq-e^{CA}\int_{\delta\wedge s}^{s}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}dt

\displaystyle=e^{CA}\Big(\log(\sigma_{\delta\wedge s})-\log(\sigma_{s})\Big).

Hence, using again
\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\leq 1
and
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0
,

\displaystyle-\int_{s\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)dt

\displaystyle\leq-e^{CA^{2}}\int_{s\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}A\sigma_{t}^{\beta-\chi}dt

\displaystyle=Ce^{CA^{2}}\Big(\sigma_{s\vee\delta}^{\beta-\chi}-\sigma_{1}^{\beta-\chi}\Big).

Combining these bounds with the assumption
\int_{\delta}^{1}\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}dt\leq C
,
we conclude

\int_{\delta}^{1}g_{1}(t)dt\leq C.

On the other hand, by Proposition
9
we have for a.e.
t\in[0,\delta]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}+h^{\top}\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)h

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}h^{\top}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)h\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0}.

By Proposition
12
,

h^{\top}\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)h\leq C\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big).

Moreover, combining Proposition
11
(with
\xi=0
, i.e. with the exponential factor)
and Lemma
12
, we obtain for
t\in[0,\delta]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}h^{\top}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)h\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0}

\displaystyle\leq-C\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\leq 0}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}>0}.

Since
\delta\leq\delta\vee s
, the bound (
71
) applies on
[0,\delta]
and yields

\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\Big)\leq e^{CA},\qquad t\in[0,\delta].

Using again Assumption
6
on
[0,\delta]
(in particular
|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|\vee\sigma_{t}^{-1}\leq A
),
we conclude that the whole bracket is bounded by a constant. In particular, for a.e.
t\in[0,\delta]
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)\leq g_{0}(t):=C\left(\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}+1\right).

Therefore,

\int_{0}^{\delta}g_{0}(t)dt\leq C.

∎

B.4.11
Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

Fix
z\in[0,1]
. Let
\delta\in(0,1)
given by Assumption

7
such that for all
t\in[0,\delta)
,
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}>0
and for all
t\in(\delta,1)

\sigma_{t}^{\prime}<0
.
Let

E_{t}:=\exp\left(CK_{t}^{2}\Big(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big)^{-\beta}\right).

Since
\big(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\big)^{-\beta}\leq\sigma_{t}^{2\beta}
and
K_{t}\leq A\sigma_{t}^{-\chi}
, we have

E_{t}\leq\exp\left(CA^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2(\beta-\chi)}\right)\leq\exp\left(CA^{2}\sigma_{\delta}^{2(\beta-\chi)}\right)=:M,

for all
t\in[0,1]
(using that
\sigma_{t}\leq\sigma_{\delta}
on
[0,1]
).

By Theorem
1
we have

\displaystyle\int_{z}^{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{z}^{z\vee\delta}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)dt

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{z\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{C}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(\left(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}\right)E_{t}+\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}\right)dt.

Now, taking
\xi_{t}=1
,

\displaystyle\int_{z\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(\left(L_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}\right)E_{t}+\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\alpha_{t}\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{z\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}dt-C\int_{z\vee\delta}^{1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}A\sigma_{t}^{\beta-\chi}dt

\displaystyle\leq C\big(A+\sigma_{z\vee\delta}^{\beta-\chi}-\sigma_{1}^{\beta-\chi}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(A+\sigma_{\delta}^{\beta-\chi}-\sigma_{1}^{\beta-\chi}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C.

On the other hand, by Theorem
1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{z}^{z\vee\delta}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{z}^{z\vee\delta}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\left(\left(L_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}\right)E_{t}+\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}A\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq C+C\int_{z}^{z\vee\delta}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}dt

\displaystyle\leq C+C\int_{0}^{\delta}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi_{t}}dt.

Now, taking
\xi_{t}=1
we have

K_{t}^{\xi}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{1-\beta\xi}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\leq C\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{1-\beta+\chi}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\leq C\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{1-\beta+\chi}},

which gives

\int_{z}^{z\vee\delta}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)dt\leq C.

Combining the two estimates (and enlarging
C
if needed) yields

\int_{z}^{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla v_{t}(x)\right)dt\leq C,

for all
z\in[0,1]
.
∎

B.4.12
Proof of Corollary
1

Proof.

Let us show that this setting satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
2
. We have that

(m_{t})_{\#}\pi=\exp(-u_{t}+a_{t}),

with
u_{t}(x)=u(x/f_{t})+d\log(f_{t})
and
a_{t}(x)=a(x/f_{t})
so
\alpha_{t}=\alpha f_{t}^{-2}
and
K_{t}=Kf_{t}^{-\beta}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\big|h^{\top}\big(m_{t}^{\prime}(z)-m_{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})\big)\big|=f_{t}^{\prime}\big|h^{\top}\big(x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}\big)\big|=\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{f_{t}}\big|h^{\top}\big(m_{t}(z)-m_{t}(z^{\prime})\big)\big|

so taking
L_{t}=\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{f_{t}}
, we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)\big)

\displaystyle\leq L_{t}^{2}\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}(Z)\big)

\displaystyle\leq L_{t}^{2}\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z).

Now,

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z))^{2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)=

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)-\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)+\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)

and applying Brascamp-Lieb inequality we have

\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)\leq(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-1}.

(72)

Furthermore, from Proposition
11
we obtain

\displaystyle|\int(h^{\top}H_{p^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}dp^{t,x}(z)-\int(h^{\top}H_{\nu^{t,x}}^{\text{Id}}(z))^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(z)|\leq C\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big),

which gives

\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)\big)\leq C\frac{L_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}\exp\big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\big).

As

\displaystyle(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}})^{-\beta}\leq\alpha_{t}^{-\beta}=\alpha^{-\beta}f_{t}^{2\beta}=\alpha^{-\beta}K^{2}K_{t}^{-2},

we deduce that

K_{t}^{2}\alpha_{t}^{-\beta}\leq A

which in particular gives

\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)\big)\leq C\frac{L_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1},

so Assumption
5
is verified. On the other hand, as
\gamma\leq\sigma_{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge f_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
f
is increasing,
\sigma
is decreasing, we have

\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}\geq(\alpha\gamma^{2})\wedge\gamma^{2}.

Then,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{L_{t}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}f_{t}}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{(\alpha\gamma^{2})\wedge\gamma^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}f_{t}f_{t}^{\prime}dt\leq\frac{f_{1}^{2}-f_{0}^{2}}{2((\alpha\gamma^{2})\wedge\gamma^{2})}\leq C.

Now, for
s=1-\gamma
we have for all
t\geq s
,

K_{t}=Kf_{t}^{-\beta}\leq Kf_{1-\gamma}^{-\beta}\leq K\sigma_{1-\gamma}^{-\beta/2}\leq K\sigma_{t}^{-\beta/2}.

Finally, as
\sigma
satisfies Assumption
6
(with
\delta=0
), we can apply Theorem
2
which gives the result.
∎

B.4.13
Proof of Corollary
2

Proof.

Let us show that the setting of Corollary
2
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
3
.
We write
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi=e^{-u_{t}+a_{t}}
with

e^{-u_{t}(x)}:=\int\varphi^{x,g_{t}}(f_{t}y)e^{-u(y)}dy,\qquad a_{t}(x):=\log\frac{\int\varphi^{x,g_{t}}(f_{t}y)e^{-u(y)+a(y)}dy}{\int\varphi^{x,g_{t}}(f_{t}y)e^{-u(y)}dy},

where
\varphi^{x,g_{t}}
is the
\mathcal{N}(x,g_{t}^{2}\mathrm{Id})
density.

By Proposition
7
we have for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\frac{\alpha}{\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}\mathrm{Id}\preceq\nabla^{2}u_{t}(x)\preceq\frac{1}{g_{t}^{2}}\mathrm{Id}.

(73)

In particular we may take

\alpha_{t}:=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}.

We now bound the
\beta
-Hölder constant of
a_{t}
.
Fix
t\in(0,1)
and define, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\nu^{t,x}(dy)\propto e^{-u(y)}\varphi^{x,g_{t}}(f_{t}y)dy,

so that
a_{t}(x)=\log\big(\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{t,x}}e^{a(Y)}\big)
.
By Lemma
8
, taking

M_{q}:=\exp\Big(2Kq+\frac{CK^{2}}{2\alpha}q^{2}\Big),

condition (
34
) in Lemma
7
holds, so applying Lemma
7
we get

|a_{t}(x)-a_{t}(x^{\prime})|\leq\begin{cases}KM_{2/(1-\beta)}^{1-\beta}\kappa_{t}^{\beta}\|x-x^{\prime}\|^{\beta},&\beta\in(0,1),\\[4.0pt]
KM_{4}^{1/2}\kappa_{t}\|x-x^{\prime}\|,&\beta=1,\end{cases}\qquad\kappa_{t}:=\dfrac{f_{t}}{\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}.

Using also the structural assumption
f_{t}+g_{t}\geq\gamma
for all
t
, we have

\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}\geq(\alpha\wedge 1)(g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2})\geq(\alpha\wedge 1)\frac{(f_{t}+g_{t})^{2}}{2}\geq(\alpha\wedge 1)\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2},

so
\kappa_{t}\leq C
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
.
Therefore the Hölder constants

K_{t}:=\sup_{x\neq x^{\prime}}\frac{|a_{t}(x)-a_{t}(x^{\prime})|}{\|x-x^{\prime}\|^{\beta}}

satisfy
K_{t}\leq C
uniformly in
t
.
Thus
(m_{t})_{\#}\pi
is
(\alpha_{t},\beta,C)
-weakly log-concave, with
C
depending only on
\alpha,\beta,K,\gamma
.

By Proposition
8
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all unit
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)\big)\leq C\bar{M}_{2}^{1/2}\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{f_{t}^{\prime 2}+\alpha g_{t}^{\prime 2}+\dfrac{(g_{t}^{\prime}f_{t}-f_{t}^{\prime}g_{t})^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}}.

Using again
f_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}\geq(\alpha\wedge 1)\gamma^{2}/2
, we get

\frac{1}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}}\leq\frac{2}{(\alpha\wedge 1)\gamma^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}}.

On the other hand,

\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1}=\sigma_{t}^{2}\frac{\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}}\geq\frac{(\alpha\wedge 1)\gamma^{2}}{2}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha\sigma_{t}^{2}+f_{t}^{2}+\alpha g_{t}^{2}}.

Combining the last two displays yields

\operatorname{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(h^{\top}m_{t}^{\prime}(Z)\big)\leq L_{t}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}+1},

with

L_{t}^{2}:=C\Big(f_{t}^{\prime 2}+\alpha g_{t}^{\prime 2}+\dfrac{(g_{t}^{\prime}f_{t}-f_{t}^{\prime}g_{t})^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big),

where
C
depends only on
\alpha,\beta,K,\gamma
(through
\bar{M}_{2}
).
Thus Assumption
5
holds.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{L_{t}}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}}dt\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{f_{t}^{\prime 2}+\alpha g_{t}^{\prime 2}}dt+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|g_{t}^{\prime}f_{t}-f_{t}^{\prime}g_{t}|}{\sigma_{t}}dt\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{0}^{1}(f^{\prime}_{t}-\sqrt{\alpha}g^{\prime}_{t})dt+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{t}^{-1+q}dt\right)

\displaystyle\leq C,

using that
|g_{t}^{\prime}f_{t}-f_{t}^{\prime}g_{t}|\leq K\sigma_{t}^{q}
and
\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{t}^{-1+q}dt\leq K
.

Since
K_{t}\leq C
uniformly, we may take
\chi=0
in Theorem
3
.
Moreover, for
t\in[0,\delta)
we have
\sigma_{t}^{\prime}>0
and by Assumption
2
we have
g_{t}\geq\gamma
.
Hence by (
73
),

\nabla^{2}u_{t}(x)\preceq\frac{1}{g_{t}^{2}}\mathrm{Id}\preceq\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\mathrm{Id},

so the third bullet of Theorem
3
holds.

All assumptions of Theorem
3
are satisfied with a constant
A

depending only on
\alpha,\beta,K,\gamma
(and on
\sigma_{\delta}
through Assumption
7
).
Therefore Theorem
3
yields for all
z\in[0,1]

\int_{z}^{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\big(\nabla v_{t}(x)\big)dt\leq C.

∎

B.4.14
Proof of Corollary
3

Proof.

We have that the drift of the probability flow-ODE is

v_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{t}\bigl(x+s(t,x)\bigr),\qquad s(t,x)=\nabla\log p_{t}(x).

Then, we need to bound the quantity

\int_{z}^{1}\frac{1}{t}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\text{Id}+\nabla s(t,x)\bigr)dt.

We use Corollary
1
. Observe that the smoothing path

X_{t}:=tY+\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\xi,\qquad Y\sim p^{\star},\ \xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\text{Id}),\ Y\perp\xi,

is a special case of the Lipman flow-matching setting (Assumption
1
) with

\pi=\gamma_{d}\otimes p^{\star},\qquad m_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})=f_{t}x_{1}\text{ with }f_{t}=t,\qquad\sigma_{t}=\sqrt{1-t^{2}}.

Indeed,
f
is increasing and
C^{1}
with
f_{0}=0
and
f_{1}=1
, while
\sigma
is decreasing and
C^{1}
on
(0,1)

with
\sigma_{0}=1
and
\sigma_{1}=0
. Moreover, choosing for instance
\gamma:=\tfrac{1}{5}
, one checks the last bullet
of Assumption
1
: since
\gamma\leq f_{1/2}=1/2
and
\gamma\leq\sigma_{1/2}=\sqrt{3}/2
, the required
non-degeneracy condition holds and

f_{1-\gamma}^{2}=(1-\gamma)^{2}=\Bigl(\frac{4}{5}\Bigr)^{2}=\frac{16}{25}\geq\sqrt{1-\Bigl(\frac{4}{5}\Bigr)^{2}}=\frac{3}{5}=\sigma_{1-\gamma}.

Therefore
(v,\pi,m,\sigma)
satisfies Assumption
1
and Corollary
1
yields

\int_{z}^{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\nabla v_{t}(x)\bigr)dt\leq C.

∎

B.5
Details for Lipschitz estimates

B.5.1
Proof of Corollary
4

Proof.

Fix
t
such that
\sigma_{t}>0
and let
\pi^{t,x}
denote the posterior law of
Z
given
X_{t}=x
.
Set

\Sigma_{t}(x):=\text{Cov}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(m_{t}(Z)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\qquad\Gamma_{t}(x):=\text{Cov}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(m^{\prime}_{t}(Z),m_{t}(Z)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

Then the identity used in the proof of Proposition
9
rewrites as

\nabla v_{t}(x)=\frac{\sigma^{\prime}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}I-\frac{\sigma^{\prime}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\Sigma_{t}(x)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Gamma_{t}(x)\qquad\text{for a.e. }t\text{ and all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(74)

Taking operator norms in (
74
) and using the triangle inequality yields

\|\nabla v_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{|\sigma^{\prime}_{t}|}{\sigma_{t}}+\frac{|\sigma^{\prime}_{t}|}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\|\Sigma_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\|\Gamma_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}.

(75)

For any unit vector
h\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

h^{\top}\Sigma_{t}(x)h=\text{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}\big(\langle h,m_{t}(Z)\rangle\big)=\int\big(\langle h,H^{p^{t,x}}_{\text{Id}}(y)\rangle\big)^{2}dp^{t,x}(y),

where
p^{t,x}
is the law of
m_{t}(Z)
under
\pi^{t,x}
.
Let
\nu^{t,x}
be the log-concave surrogate posterior for which Brascamp-Lieb gives

\int\big(\langle h,H^{\nu^{t,x}}_{\text{Id}}(y)\rangle\big)^{2}d\nu^{t,x}(y)\leq(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-1}.

(76)

Moreover Proposition
11
controls the discrepancy between
p^{t,x}
and
\nu^{t,x}
at the level of second moments:
for
\xi=0
it yields, uniformly in
x
and
h
,

\Big|h^{\top}\Big(\int H_{\text{Id}}^{p^{t,x}}\otimes H_{\text{Id}}^{p^{t,x}}dp^{t,x}-\int H_{\text{Id}}^{\nu^{t,x}}\otimes H_{\text{Id}}^{\nu^{t,x}}d\nu^{t,x}\Big)h\Big|\leq C(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Big).

Combining with (
76
) and taking the supremum over
h
gives the operator bound

\|\Sigma_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}\leq C(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-1}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Big)=C\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Big).

(77)

For unit vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, Cauchy-Schwarz gives

|u^{\top}\Gamma_{t}(x)v|=\big|\text{Cov}_{\pi^{t,x}}(\langle u,m^{\prime}_{t}(Z)\rangle,\langle v,m_{t}(Z)\rangle)\big|\leq\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}(\langle u,m^{\prime}_{t}(Z)\rangle)}\sqrt{\text{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}(\langle v,m_{t}(Z)\rangle)}.

Assumption
5
exactly provides a uniform bound on the first variance term:

\text{Var}_{\pi^{t,x}}(\langle u,m^{\prime}_{t}(Z)\rangle)\leq L_{t}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}},\qquad\forall x,\forall u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

The second variance term is controlled by (
77
). Taking suprema in
u,v
yields

\|\Gamma_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}\leq CL_{t}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Big).

(78)

Plugging (
77
) and (
78
) into (
75
) gives: for a.e.
t
with
\sigma_{t}>0
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla v_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{|\sigma^{\prime}_{t}|}{\sigma_{t}}+C\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\exp\Big(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Big)\left(\frac{|\sigma^{\prime}_{t}|}{\sigma_{t}}+L_{t}\right).

(79)

∎

B.5.2
A refined variant of Corollary
4

Corollary 14
.

Let
v:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form (
11
) with
\sigma\in C^{1}_{\text{a.e.}}([0,1],[0,1])
and
(m_{t})_{t}
satisfying Assumptions
4
and
5
.
Assume moreover that, in the weak log-concavity decomposition

(m_{t})_{\#}\pi(x)=\exp\bigl(-u_{t}(x)+a_{t}(x)\bigr),

the convex part
u_{t}
satisfies for some
A_{t}>0

\nabla^{2}u_{t}(x)\preceq A_{t}\text{Id}\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
independent of the dimension such that for every

t\in[0,1]
with
\sigma_{t}>0
and every
\xi\in\{0,1\}
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla v_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Biggl(C\exp\Bigl(CK_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{-2})^{-\beta}\Bigr)\Bigl(L_{t}+\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}\Bigr)+\sigma_{t}|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|A_{t}\Biggr).

Compared to Corollary
4
, this estimate improves the dependence on the explicit Gaussian term:
the contribution
\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}}
disappears and it is replaced by the finer term

\frac{|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{t}}(K_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\beta})^{\xi}

which can be much smaller when the perturbation is small at the noise scale.
The additional term
\sigma_{t}|\sigma_{t}^{\prime}|A_{t}
reflects the upper curvature bound on the convex part
u_{t}
, and it comes from a direct covariance estimate on the log-concave comparison posterior.

Proof.

We have from Proposition
9

\displaystyle\|\nabla v_{t}(x)\|_{\text{op}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}h^{\top}\nabla v_{t}(x)h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sup_{h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}h^{\top}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)^{\otimes 2}d\pi^{t,x}(z)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\int H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}^{\prime}}(z)\otimes H_{\pi^{t,x}}^{m_{t}}(z)d\pi^{t,x}(z)\right)h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{A_{1},-A_{2}\}

for

A_{1}:=\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}}\text{Id}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int(H_{\text{Id}}^{p^{t,x}})^{\otimes 2}dp^{t,x}\right)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Bigl\|\int H^{m_{t}^{\prime}}_{\pi^{t,x}}\otimes H^{m_{t}}_{\pi^{t,x}}d\pi^{t,x}\Bigr\|_{\text{op}}

and
